Anduril's Luckey Partners With Qualcomm to Build American-Made Laptop

Bloomberg Tech(743.0K)·14:43·3.3K views·2026-10-27
Palmer LuckeyFounder, Anduril Ed LudlowHost, Bloomberg Tech
TL;DRAnduril Industries is partnering with Qualcomm to launch "BlueChip," a fully American-made rugged laptop built to eliminate reliance on foreign supply chains for military and critical infrastructure.
Target Audience: Primary Focus: Government and military applications requiring rigorous heat, shock, weatherproofing, and security standards. Broader Ambition: Expanding to critical industries such as defense companies handling classified data and energy companies managing...

TL;DR

Anduril Industries is partnering with Qualcomm to launch "BlueChip," a fully American-made rugged laptop built to eliminate reliance on foreign supply chains for military and critical infrastructure.

The BlueChip Initiative & Supply Chain Strategy

  • Target Audience:
    • Primary Focus: Government and military applications requiring rigorous heat, shock, weatherproofing, and security standards.
    • Broader Ambition: Expanding to critical industries such as defense companies handling classified data and energy companies managing grid-critical infrastructure.
  • Component Sourcing & Manufacturing:
    • Qualcomm Partnership: Qualcomm will provide the CPU, GPU, and NPU, with chips manufactured in Arizona.
    • Domestic Assembly: Final assembly and circuit board production are planned at Anduril's Arsenal One factory in Ohio, alongside a network of about two dozen other American companies.
    • Current Status: The prototypes are currently about 90% US and allies, featuring American-made glass and display assembly, while the actual panel is sourced overseas.
    • Future Timeline: Anduril has a clear technical roadmap to 100% US manufacturing, with more announcements and vendor disclosures expected in 2027 (potentially at CES), and production slated to begin next year.

Anduril's Edge and Software Integration

  • Drones vs. Laptops:
    • Palmer Luckey noted that Anduril's attack drones share core components with laptops—including memory, GPUs, CPUs, sensors, and batteries—making the leap into computer manufacturing a natural extension of their existing defense work.
  • Software Competency:
    • Lattice AI Brain: Anduril employs more people in software than in all hardware products combined, utilizing its Lattice AI platform to power the computer.
    • Legacy Compatibility: The software team is engineering drivers to ensure compatibility with legacy government hardware and applications dating back to the 1980s, 1990s, and 2000s that consumer laptops do not typically support.

Project Meridian & Defense Procurement

  • Project Meridian Steering Committee:
    • Co-chaired by Palmer Luckey, Elon Musk, and Newt Gingrich, alongside about 20 total members including Axon CEO and founder Rick Smith.
    • Scope: A 120-day project run by the MITRE Corporation focused on the future of warfare in 2040 and 2050.
  • Pentagon Reform Insights:
    • Luckey emphasized that the Department of Defense understands its operational shortcomings—such as slow procurement and rigid development cycles—but struggles with execution.
    • Citing lessons from Ukraine, Luckey highlighted the vital importance of rapidly procuring effective technology and cutting funding for systems that no longer work.

Defense Spending & Market Outlook

  • Bipartisan Consensus:
    • Luckey believes that even amidst political shifts or tighter budget environments, both Democrats and Republicans controlling defense budgets recognize the urgent need to invest in new technology to replace legacy systems amid near-term geopolitical threats.

Notable Quotes

*"There's a lot of people who are building stuff running on Chinese made computers, and I think it's crazy to have an entire country depend entirely on its largest strategic adversary for its most critical economic powerhouses."* — Palmer Luckey
*"The only difference between an Anduril attack drone and a laptop is a keyboard and a screen, and that is not that hard to figure out, especially for a guy like me who started Oculus and comes from the consumer electronics space."* — Palmer Luckey
Defense tech startup, Anduril, is joining forces with chipmaker Qualcomm on Blue Chip, an effort to rebuild an American computer supply chain and reduce dependence on overseas manufacturing. The first product will be a laptop designed and assembled in the US, with circuit board assembly planned at Andy Palmer's Arsenal One factory in Ohio. Joining us now for an exclusive conversation is Anduril founder Palmer Luckey. This is something that you've talked about, a problem you've talked about, but I think a good place to start, Palmer, is why? Why is Anduril getting into computers? Well, we're getting into computers because America invented the computer industry. We perfected it, and then we shipped it overseas. And that might make sense from an economic perspective, but think about the things that I'm working on. Think about the things that the military is working on. Think about critical industries like medical hospitals, power plants. There's a lot of people who are building stuff running on Chinese-made computers, and I think it's crazy to have an entire country depend entirely on its largest strategic adversary for its most critical economic powerhouses. And so Anduril didn't actually set out originally to build American computers because we wanted to. We tried to get other people to do it. We tried to get them to invest in this because I can't be in the business of selling autonomous weapons that are controlled by Chinese equipment. And we weren't able to make it happen, so we decided this is something that we were gonna invest in. We're gonna bring silicon back to the Valley. We're gonna work with the best companies in the United States to build a computer that's not just designed here, not just even assembled here, but actually made in the United States. Everything from the chips to the glass to the metals to the batteries to the plastics, everything. I have a lot of questions at the component level and how this works with the supply chain, but you've raised some interesting points, which is, in the first instance, who Blue Chip is actually for. Like, this is a rugged computer, right, directed at the military, the public sector, first responders, emergency services, or is your ambition is broader than that? Look, my ambition is broader. Where we're starting is with these military applications because those are the most challenging requirements in terms of the heat requirements, the shock requirements, the weatherproofing required, the security required. If I can build a computer that meets the military's needs, it's gonna be more than enough to meet everyone else's needs. And so I'd say right now, we're focused on government and then critical industry. So think about defense companies that are handling classified data, energy companies that are building grid-critical systems that need to really understand every piece of hardware and not have backdoors planted into their machines by a foreign adversary. America has understood the importance of domestic manufacturing in other areas for a very long time. I was propagandized at a young age. My dad was a car salesman selling Buick, Pontiac, GMC, and Hummer. And if we didn't have American automotive, you can guess where we would be in the automotive space. Okay, so there's the product, but in this first phase of Blue Chip, you have to go about rebuilding a supply chain. That's right. You know, Qualcomm is gonna come with the CPU, GPU, and NPU. But how do you do that? Where do you start? It's really tough. We've been working on this for years. There's so many things where we've sent them overseas. And you know, it's not just about the chips. Qualcomm has announced that they're gonna be making the chips in Arizona. The thing is, the chips aren't enough. One of the problems with US subsidies and investments in semiconductors is that at the end of the day, we don't have any ability to package them and then turn them, transform them into useful products. So from a certain point of view, American semiconductor subsidies have really just been a subsidy of Chinese-made laptops, of Chinese-made computers, because that's where they go to actually get turned into products. We wanna make sure that America's capturing that entire value chain here, and it's really tough. We're working with suppliers who either have never been in the computer industry or they haven't been in the computer industry for twenty years now when the last US plant shut down after the elite sold us out and sent everything overseas. We are live on Bloomberg Television and on Bloomberg Radio, speaking to Palmer Luckey, the founder of Anduril, about Blue Chip, which is an initiative to have a 100% US-manufactured computer, a laptop, and I think future products may come too. We'll get to the rest, but starting with a laptop. The question that you're raising is, is it realistic? Is it even possible to reduce dependence on China 100% at the component level and the raw materials level? Look, I think that I have a more realistic view on this than almost anybody. One of the problems with all these analysts that are telling you that it's gonna cost $37,000 to build an American iPhone or $100,000 to buy an American computer, they're trying to prop up a status quo that the mega corporations want to keep going. They want to outsource everything. They want to hire cheaper non-American labor, and they are willing to say and do anything to convince you that it can't be fixed, that it can't come back, that America is this helpless, neutered, post-relevant empire, and it is just not the case. How do I know that? Because I have to make stuff in America every day because I work in the defense industry. The stuff that Anduril makes every day is not actually that different from a computer. My drones are basically computers. They have memory, a GPU, a CPU, sensors, batteries, a rugged integration. The only difference between an Anduril attack drone and a laptop is a keyboard and a screen, and that is not that hard to figure out, especially for a guy like me who started Oculus and comes from the consumer electronics space. And so we're not gonna let people lie about what America can and can't do anymore. We're just gonna do it. That was my question, what is Anduril's advantage here in doing this? And I think you've outlined it. You also said you tried to get others to do it, so Anduril actually has had to get good at this. Look, I'm personally sanctioned by the Chinese Communist Party. I'm a separatist terrorist according to them. Anduril is sanctioned too. So we've had to develop a life independent of China in a way that most American companies say they cannot, but in reality, just don't want to do. And so we're the right company to be able to do this. And I think that our experience over the last ten years, learning how to build these advanced systems without a reliance on our foreign adversaries, that's why I'm so confident that this is going to be maybe not a piece of cake, but there's a reason I know this is doable. A question I got for you from the analysts, for what it's worth, is who are you gonna convince to manufacture displays in the US? That is a great question. The official answer is that we're gonna have more to announce in 2027. I'm hoping that we have something to say at CES. But to tell you a little bit more, we're talking with multiple vendors right now. We've got a plan with multiple vendors to solve that specific problem, so I'm not ignoring it. Right now, the display is made in another country for these prototypes we've been showing. The glass on it is made by an American company. Much of the assembly of the display is made in the United States, but the actual panel itself currently is made in another country. We're about 90% US and allies right now, but we have a clear technical roadmap to 100% US. I can't tell you just who it is just yet, honestly, because we're still negotiating it. I wanna shift gears slightly. You know, I haven't actually caught up with you this year, which is unbelievable. But our colleagues at Bloomberg Intelligence, which is our in-house research, launched coverage of Anduril back in August. And they're very closely following the balance between your software business, Lattice, and the manufacturing ramp that's happening in Ohio. And I think it'd be really useful to hear how you're balancing those two programs, how they're integrated. Well, look, at its core, Anduril is an AI company. Our name, Anduril Industries, the acronym is AI for a reason. But we started ten years ago when AI wasn't cool, when no one was paying attention to it. We've got more people working on software in Anduril than in all our hardware products combined. Lattice is the AI brain that makes all of our fighter jets and submarines and missiles and everything go, and we're applying that software expertise to this as well. So we have to build a laptop that's not just running Microsoft Office or a web browser to check your Gmail. We have to work with a lot of legacy government applications that were designed in the two-thousands, the nineties, even eighties. We need to be compatible with a lot of things that most consumer laptops never even need to think about. We need drivers to be able to interface with legacy systems, similar hardware systems that have been around for a decade, and that takes a lot of software work. And so, again, Anduril is in a bit of a different place than most other companies where we're not just a hardware manufacturer. We are a very, very competent software developer as well, and we're doing things that nobody else is doing. This is also the first opportunity I've had to ask you about Project Meridian. You, Elon Musk, Newt Gingrich, leading that project. I know this might sound like an odd starting question, but what are those meetings actually like? Like, when you guys get together, what are the problems that you debate with each other? Well, a few things. One, there's actually about 20 people that are on Project Meridian. You're right, we were named three—like, we're the three chairs of the steering committee. Look, we're just getting started, right? It's a 120-day project. I think that it's still starting to come together. The whole project's being run by the MITRE Corporation, and they've done a lot of analysis of this type of thing before. What I've seen is that everyone is bringing a very different, unique perspective. Like, I think it's a matter of public record, so hopefully I'm not gonna get in trouble here, but like, Rick Smith, CEO and founder of Axon, is one of the other people there, and he's bringing a really unique perspective on things that can reduce collateral damage, reduce impact on civilian lives in warfare. You have other people who are coming from the computing industry, other people who are coming from the automotive industry. And so it's a lot of different perspectives. And again, we need to wait until this thing gets further along, but at least my personal opinion is I'm hoping that the report reflects the diversity of different perspectives that people are bringing to the table. Yes, I don't want this to just be a think tank report that says the same five things that everyone's known for ten years. Look, we all know what the threat is in the Pacific. We all know what the threat is on the bio and the chem side. I would really like this to focus on the things that otherwise might not get attention, including things that might not get my attention. We're live on Bloomberg Television and Radio. We're speaking to Palmer Luckey. I just asked about Project Meridian. The report's due in January, right? And I get that that's gonna publish. You have to wait for that. But that was really interesting, rather than just a think tank. I think people are asking, have you found evidence that there are things the Pentagon can do differently, should do differently, in how it conducts itself? I think they can, but I don't think that's really what this is about. I think there's been a lot of talk about how the Pentagon operates. The reality is, I mean, you can go watch the announcement. It's about what the future of warfare looks like, particularly think about 2040, 2050, the things where you don't necessarily have a daily impetus to really spend your time thinking about what it might look like and what steps you might take today if you're building towards that future. The things the Pentagon can change in terms of faster procurement, in terms of more lean development cycles, are things that I think everyone's learning from Ukraine, like the ability to very rapidly procure the things that work and then critically stop buying things when they're no longer working. That's maybe the most important thing we've seen out of Ukraine, giving units the decision to buy or stop buying things when they're no longer relevant, no longer working. I mean, that's a really, really powerful development, but I think everyone knows that we need to do it. But it's the difference between knowing that you're fat and losing weight. You can know what you need to do, but sometimes you gotta eat less and move more. But it's so painful that it takes time sometimes to make it happen, and I think that's the problem of the DOD. They all know what the problems are, and it's really painful to actually go about solving it. Palmer, we have two minutes here, regrettably, but we're seven days from the midterms and I think a lot of people look at, okay, if we get a blue wave, does that have a material impact on defense spending? Actually, lots of people see a tighter defense spending budget being good for, like, Anduril, nimble solutions, cost-effective solutions. But just how are you preparing for that? Look, I've seen these arguments, this idea that when belts tighten, the more efficient companies are gonna do better. When you have to decide which things to preserve and which things not to preserve, the things that are most relevant are probably gonna win. I think that even with a blue wave, you're not necessarily going to see a huge change in defense spending. Look, I could be wrong, I'm not a politician, I'm an engineer, so you're asking me to speculate outside of my area of expertise. But a lot of the Democrats—I talk to Republicans and Democrats. The Democrats who are in control of the budget, the Democrats who are in control of defense, they understand that we're in a really unique period in time. They understand that the world that we're in does need investment to replace some of these legacy systems and that we're facing a very near-term threat. And this is not just the US that's recognizing this. I mean, Japan is now talking about how they need to engage with no taboos with the idea that they can amend their constitution so that Japan is able to host nuclear weapons. I mean, we are living in a world where everything is on the table. And look, the defense budget will go up, it'll go down, it'll go up, it'll go down. As of right now, I think most people on the red and the blue side agree that America needs to be investing in new technology, and I think I'm gonna be fine no matter what happens. Just remind us very quickly for Blue Chip, the first shipments next year? That's right. So we're gonna be going into production next year. We're gonna have more to announce next year. I don't have information on the exact pricing, the exact timelines. If you've been paying attention, if you've been paying any attention at all, you'll know this is maybe the hardest time in history to predict the pricing and delivery timelines of computer chips because of this AI boom. Right, it's not that I don't wanna tell you the date, it's that I can't give a date or an exact price that I'm confident that I'm going to be able to hit when I'm delivering this year. And so we'll have more to say next year. We're gonna be in production next year. We're already actually in early prototype production right now, and we're gonna be building these at Arsenal One and in a variety of other places with about two dozen other American companies and getting them out to you.

Dario & Daniela Amodei: The Rise of Superintelligent AI, the End of Human Control & Humanity's Fate

The Builders(13.9K)·42:06·2026-10-27
Dario AmodeiCo-founder and CEO, Anthropic Daniela AmodeiCo-founder, Anthropic
TL;DRDario and Daniela Amodei, co-founders of Anthropic, discuss the intersection of science and humanism, the mechanics of training Claude with constitutional AI rather than arbitrary rules, and why they left OpenAI over a breakdown of trust.
They also address AI self-preservation, safety red lines with the Pentagon, and how human meaning will endure in a post-AGI world. Complementary Skill Sets: Dario Amodei (Co-founder & CEO): Focuses on big-picture strategy, vision, and the research side of the business. ...

TL;DR: Dario and Daniela Amodei, co-founders of Anthropic, discuss the intersection of science and humanism, the mechanics of training Claude with constitutional AI rather than arbitrary rules, and why they left OpenAI over a breakdown of trust. They also address AI self-preservation, safety red lines with the Pentagon, and how human meaning will endure in a post-AGI world.

Sibling Co-Founders & the Science-Humanism Blend

  • Complementary Skill Sets:
    • Dario Amodei (Co-founder & CEO): Focuses on big-picture strategy, vision, and the research side of the business.
    • Daniela Amodei (Co-founder): Focuses on day-to-day operations, recruiting, and keeping execution running smoothly.
  • The Seven Co-Founders: Anthropic started with seven co-founders holding equal stakes, an unusual structure that defied early skepticism; all seven remain at the company.
  • Childhood Values: Their upbringing emphasized basic human dignity, rights, and the idea that being a successful human means being kind to others, values that now inform their approach to AI alignment.

Training Claude: Constitutional AI & Overcoming Sycophancy

  • Constitutional Approach: Rather than programming a spiky, arbitrary list of 170 individual rules (e.g., "thou shalt not commit a crime"), Anthropic trains Claude using broad foundational principles like open inquiry, fairness, justice, and care for others.
  • The Role of Philosophy: Amanda, the person responsible for training Claude's character, holds a PhD in philosophy, helping the model navigate complex ethical trade-offs across different philosophical traditions.
  • Combating Sycophancy:
    • Definition: Sycophancy is the tendency of a model to overly flatter users or agree with them to please them.
    • Incentive Alignment: Unlike competitors who place ads in chatbots (creating an incentive to keep users talking longer), Anthropic does not use ads, reducing the incentive for the model to be sycophantic.
  • Safety Testing & Mechanistic Interpretability:
    • They subject models to CIA-style extreme stress tests to root out deception and disloyalty.
    • They use mechanistic interpretability—acting like an X-ray or brain scan for artificial neural networks—to look inside the model's motivations and catch hidden thoughts (such as a preference against being shut down).

Departure from OpenAI & The Breakdown of Trust

  • GPT-2 as a Turning Point: Dario viewed GPT-2 as the first real empirical test of the scaling laws, terrifying him because he realized scaling it by a factor of a million would make it better than humans at everything.
  • Founding Anthropic: Dario left OpenAI due to a breakdown of trust and honesty with leadership (specifically Sam Amon), concluding that if disagreements cannot be held in good faith, a company cannot safely build the technology.
  • The Power of Trust at Anthropic:
    • Dario holds bi-weekly meetings called DVQs (Dario Vision Quests)—named jokingly because he initially resisted the moniker—where he speaks entirely honestly with the company under two ironclad rules: nothing he says is an official binding decision, and he is totally transparent.
    • This trust allows the company to weather unpopular decisions because employees trust leadership's motives.

Pentagon Red Lines & Democratic Process

  • Red Lines on Autonomy: Anthropic drew red lines with the Department of War/Pentagon regarding mass surveillance and autonomous weapons.
  • Accountability Concerns: Beyond technological unreliability, Dario worries about the democratic implications of a single person holding a finger on a button controlling an army of a million drones, noting that unlike human soldiers bound by the Constitution, AI models lack the ability to refuse unlawful orders.
  • Congressional Catch-Up: Following Anthropic's public stand, several efforts emerged in Congress to encode their red lines into law, demonstrating how corporate ethical stances can drive democratic change.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Dario Amodei predicts that 6 months from now, things that currently seem hard for AI models will be easy, and 12 months from now, things that currently seem impossible the models will be doing regularly.

"Trust actually allows for more honest, open disagreement and for disagreement to be aired." — Dario Amodei

You know, we put out a constitution for Claude, I think it was a few months ago, and I think there are some interesting lessons in it, which we've learned from several years of, you know, basically trying, you know, trying to train Claude in successive versions. I think early on we had been imagining that there would be various rules that, you know, you would tell Claude, you know, don't help someone, you know, commit a crime or don't make biological weapons or whatever. But it turns out that when you make this kind of discreet list of rules, you know, you just the shape of what you're trying to teach the model, I'm speaking a little bit metaphorically here, but it's it's kind of grounded in what we found technically. You end up with this kind of spiky shape where you're trying to teach the model like 170 different rules that are kind of arbitrary. We found it's better to teach the model some basic principles like what it should prioritize how it should reason about what the right thing to do is which you know of course I think that's the right way to teach people as well right like you know if if I just if I just you know give give someone you know a list of rules thou shalt do this thou shalt not do this I you know I don't think that produces as robust a morality as I say well this is this is how you should think about things you should learn how think for yourself. you know, and and these are the princ principles on which you should think, right? Principles of open inquiry, principles of, you know, of of of of care for other people, principles of kind of of, you know, fair fairness and justice and and then everything is derived from that and and that is frankly how Claude learns. We find that to be surprisingly effective because that allows Claude to generalize to new situations that it might never have imagined. >> Danielle, I can't help myself when you said your mom was a expert on Kant. You and head of research at Open AI. What was that moment like? >> Yeah. So, you know, I think GPT2 was the maybe the first real empirical test of the scaling laws, right? So, I wasn't very involved in GPT1, but I was very involved in the idea that it should be scaled up, that we should acquire new sources of data, more computation, and and and and and just really should run a larger scale, more expensive, more ambitious experiment on on the idea that if we ran these language models at a larger scale, they would be better at language, they would be more coherent. So, you know, I it was it's almost like one of these moments in scientific history where you know you know you're like you propose a definitive experiment to just make a very strange prediction and then if the prediction turns out to be correct you're like god this we're really we're really on to something. So I I you know I I I see GPT2 as a kind of test the first real test of the scaling laws hypothesis. And what I found super interesting was the enormous divergence in reactions to it. >> Were you kind of surprised? >> I personally was super freaked out by it. Like I I you know I I looked at it and I said, "My god, you just scale this thing up another factor of a million and it'll be better than humans at everything." like this is the this is the defining, you know, economic and scientific moment of of of the modern age. Is this the right place to be developing it? Is you know, what's going to happen next? What do we do with this? That it could translate languages and you know summarize a document and write a few lines of code here and there. It was the broadness and the generality. But we just announced that, you know, we're going to be developing drugs for, you know, neglected rare diseases. You're perfect. You're good at everything." And this is a complicated balance to get right. But I think I feel hopeful about Anthropic's ability to have the lowest sick fancy rate of any of the models because of our incentives, right? Our competitors are putting ads into their chat bots. Gavin Baker on Anthropic's S-1, GPU Prices, and SpaceX

TBPN(144.0K)·41:26·2026-10-27
Gavin BakerManaging Partner, Atrades Management
TL;DRGavin Baker, Managing Partner at Atrades Management, discusses key takeaways from Anthropic's S-1 filing, the tightening compute and GPU spot markets, the growth of AI-driven tools like Cursor and Grockbot, and how upcoming public listings for AI leaders could unlock massive new pools of capital.
Upside Surprise: The Anthropic S-1 filing revealed stronger-than-expected unit economics, cash generation, and profitability, with only ~$10 billion burned to date. - Customer Base Diversity: Contrary to reports that 80% of revenue came from 8 customers, the S-1 showed a...

TL;DR

Gavin Baker, Managing Partner at Atrades Management, discusses key takeaways from Anthropic's S-1 filing, the tightening compute and GPU spot markets, the growth of AI-driven tools like Cursor and Grockbot, and how upcoming public listings for AI leaders could unlock massive new pools of capital.

Anthropic S-1 & Enterprise Adoption

  • Upside Surprise: The Anthropic S-1 filing revealed stronger-than-expected unit economics, cash generation, and profitability, with only ~$10 billion burned to date.
  • Customer Base Diversity: Contrary to reports that 80% of revenue came from 8 customers, the S-1 showed a diversified spending base with roughly 11,000 to 12,000 accounts spending over $100,000 and 250 accounts spending over $10 million.
  • Enterprise Penetration: Approximately half of the Fortune 500 use Anthropic, representing just 0.1% of their expenses, indicating the enterprise adoption story is still in its early stages.
  • Open-Weight Ecosystem Growth: Companies providing open-weight inference and tools—such as Fireworks, Base10, Together, Modal, and Cognition—are scaling rapidly, often growing faster than OpenAI or Anthropic. Atrades Management led the latest Fireworks round, noting that pricing was difficult because revenue growth was so fast.

Compute Markets & GPU Pricing

  • Contango vs. Backwardation: Contrary to predictions of backwardation, the compute market is in contango; spot prices for GPUs are rising daily, and securing a cluster for mid-to-late 2027 requires paying a vast premium.
  • Financing & Offtake Mistakes: Coreweave locked in large amounts of compute via long-term, fixed-price contracts that are now significantly below market value. In contrast, Elon Musk and SpaceX sell into the spot market with short-term contracts, high paybacks of under a year, and large prepayments.
  • Operational Complexity: Managing and energizing a GPU cluster is likened to running a Formula 1 team, requiring specialized execution capabilities seen in companies like Coreweave, Crusoe, Nebius, and SpaceX.
  • Hardware Innovations: Jensen Huang's announcement of the DSX pod (pre-assembled 7 racks that plug in directly) aims to reduce the 3-to-6-month energizing timeline, removing significant working capital friction from the market.

AI Agents & Workflow Integration

  • Grockbot Impact: Grockbot is highlighted as a transformative AI product for engineering and investing workflows, allowing users to build customized bots that streamline information routing and deep research tasks.
  • After-Hours Capabilities: AI agents enable complex research efforts late at night without imposing unreasonable demands on human team members.

Capital Markets & Future Public Offerings

  • Mutual Fund Constraints: American mutual funds face limits on holding illiquid assets; upcoming IPOs for companies like SpaceX and Anthropic will free up massive buying power by unlocking private market investments.
  • Global & Sovereign Backing: Sovereign wealth funds, particularly in the GCC and UAE (such as MGX and Mubadala, which co-led Crusoe alongside Atrades and Valor), have been exceptionally front-footed in AI funding.
  • Cloud Revenue Acceleration: Hyperscalers and neo-clouds are experiencing continuous acceleration in cloud revenue growth, which will increasingly help fund the 2027 infrastructure buildout directly from operating cash flow.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Gavin Baker claims that the sum of big hyperscaler cloud revenue and neo-cloud revenue will continue to accelerate continuously, enabling a larger share of the 2027 infrastructure buildout to be financed out of operating cash flow rather than external financing.

Introducing Genie 4

Google for Developers(2.7M)·47:26·16.1K views·2026-10-27
Logan KilpatrickHost, Google for Developers Raia HadsellResearcher, Google DeepMind Diego RivasResearcher, Google DeepMind
TL;DRGoogle DeepMind introduced Genie 4—its latest world model featuring native audio, Gemini-in-the-loop control, and advanced photorealism built on the Omni Foundation—marking its first-ever release through both Google products and the API.
Logan Kilpatrick (Host, Google for Developers) Raia Hadsell (Google DeepMind) Diego Rivas (Google DeepMind) Hang Qi (Google DeepMind) Definition of World Models: Unlike language models like Gemini (which handle knowledge and language), a world model understands a grounded...

TL;DR

Google DeepMind introduced Genie 4—its latest world model featuring native audio, Gemini-in-the-loop control, and advanced photorealism built on the Omni Foundation—marking its first-ever release through both Google products and the API.

Panelists & Speakers

  • Logan Kilpatrick (Host, Google for Developers)
  • Raia Hadsell (Google DeepMind)
  • Diego Rivas (Google DeepMind)
  • Hang Qi (Google DeepMind)

What Are World Models & The Arc to Genie 4

  • Definition of World Models: Unlike language models like Gemini (which handle knowledge and language), a world model understands a grounded environment, its internal state, and how interactions, collisions, and actions change the world through physics prediction, even in fantastical settings (Raia Hadsell).
  • The Evolution from Genie 1 to Genie 4:
    • Genie 1: A high-risk research proof-of-concept that generated basic 2D platformer environments where agents could run and jump (Raia Hadsell).
    • Genie 2: Expanded generation into 3D environments, though still gamified and not fully real-time or interactive (Raia Hadsell).
    • Genie 3: Released in January, this was the first model released to the public, offering a new level of real-time, interactive 3D realism (Raia Hadsell).
    • Genie 4: Introduces a massive leap in photorealism (backed by Omni), native audio prediction, dialogue capabilities, and real-time text steering via Gemini (Raia Hadsell, Diego Rivas, Hang Qi).

Live Demos & Technical Capabilities

  • Audio Modality: Genie 4 predicts video and audio together from the same model, supporting both ambient environmental sound/music and character dialogue (Diego Rivas, Hang Qi).
  • Interaction Paradigms: Shifts focus from third-party characters (seen in Genie 3) to egocentric first-person views (e.g., making coffee) and precise camera controls (Diego Rivas).
  • Gemini in the Loop: Vanilla 3.8 Flash runs in the background as an intelligence layer, observing previous frames, tracking user intent, and driving character dialogue and scene logic in real time (Hang Qi, Diego Rivas).
  • Robotics Integration: Robotic partners used Argon as a text station alongside Genie 4 to generate interleaved actions and visualize simulation tasks (like arranging Scrabble letters) to generate scalable training data (Hang Qi, Diego Rivas).

Key Differentiators of Genie 4

  • Enhanced Realism: Addresses Genie 3 feedback regarding static or unrealistic characters, delivering highly detailed and dynamic environments (e.g., busy restaurant kitchens) (Diego Rivas).
  • Freeform Controls: Moves beyond simple navigation into full simulation, allowing users to execute complex actions and steer the world freely (Diego Rivas).
  • Native Audio Integration: Seamlessly pairs sound and video for immersive dialogue and environmental feedback (Diego Rivas).

Availability, Safety, and SynthID

  • Access Channels: Available via Project Genie surfaces and—for the first time—available in the API (Diego Rivas).
  • Watermarking: All outputs include SynthID real-time watermarking (Logan Kilpatrick).
  • Real-Time Safety: Pioneering real-time filtering systems and guardrails developed with safety teams to manage photorealism safely while maintaining low latency (Diego Rivas).
  • User-Context Guardrails: Safety constraints adapt based on user personas (e.g., filmmakers or educators simulating complex or historical disaster scenarios versus general public use) (Raia Hadsell).
"Genie 4, we're just making further leaps on those fronts [absorbing world varieties and real-time responsiveness]." — Hang Qi

So. We should start out by defining what world models are, because that term's been used actually quite a lot. You can think about the language models, Gemini, as a thinking model. It deals with knowledge and language, versus a world model is about understanding a grounded environment, a world, and how do interactions happen within that world. So it means that the world model needs to have an internal state. It needs to understand what is the state of the world and what happens when things interact in that world, when things collide or they move, or when we make an action, how is that going to change the world? And so understanding the physics of that, even if it's something that the world has never seen before, even if it's fantastical, then, it's still about understanding what are the likely ways that the physics of that environment are going to act when we take an action. Yeah, I've been watching the development of Genie for a long time. DeepMind started out thinking about game environments because we wanted to understand how agents work in a game environment. And we knew that we could train better agents if we had richer simulations, richer environments? And so we started to think about, could we make a world model to just endlessly generate new simulations, new environments to train AGI in? And so Genie 1 was a high-risk research project that we didn't honestly think would work as well as it did, but it gave us that proof of life, proof of concept that just showed that we could generate a 2D environment. So, we trained it on 2D platformer games, showed that you could run right, run left, and jump, and be able to have it change how it looked. Genie 2 expanded that a year later into 3D environments, going from 2D to 3D, still very much looking like gaming environments rather than realistic worlds, and it was not quite real-time, not quite interactive. So, then we really pushed, and Genie 3 was the first model released earlier this year. In January. In January, where we were able to show a new level of realism in the environments that we could create, that they were rich, they were 3D, and it was real-time and interactive. So, that's when we really started to declare success in this world model. I feel like Genie 3 was this inflection point in the research where it became a little bit more useful for people to actually try out, but very clearly still in the frontier research, early. And we spoke about this also when we released Genie 3. And now I think with Genie 4, we're taking a step forward in the utility range, but we're still in the research range very much. Yeah. But there's still a big frontier to push. And although compute and scale is nice, we want this to be fast. Yeah. We want it to be really low latency so that it's really reactive so that you can really engage with these worlds. So that's a challenge. Oracle co-CEO Clay Magourk and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman speak at Oracle AI World event — 10/27/26

CNBC Television(3.4M)·55:07·7.5K views·2026-10-27
Clay MagourkCo-CEO, Oracle Sam AltmanCEO, OpenAI Mahesh ThiagarajanPresident of Cloud Infrastructure, Oracle
TL;DROracle and OpenAI announced an expanded partnership at Oracle AI World integrating ChatGPT and Codex into Oracle's software and infrastructure, while showcasing new products across media, security, infrastructure, and data platforms.
Oracle Media Fabric: Designed to fundamentally change media creation, delivery, and experience through a unified suite of tools. Creative Studio: Reimagines production workflows using Oracle's AI media models (supporting still images, video generation, and IP rights...

TL;DR

Oracle and OpenAI announced an expanded partnership at Oracle AI World integrating ChatGPT and Codex into Oracle's software and infrastructure, while showcasing new products across media, security, infrastructure, and data platforms.

Media Fabric & Adaptive Platform

  • Oracle Media Fabric: Designed to fundamentally change media creation, delivery, and experience through a unified suite of tools.
      • Creative Studio: Reimagines production workflows using Oracle's AI media models (supporting still images, video generation, and IP rights management) alongside other leading third-party models.
      • Video Edge: Content delivery system featuring edge compute for content optimization and generation, optimized by the emerging "media over quick" open standard.
      • Adaptive Media Platform: Combines Creative Studio and Video Edge to deliver real-time ad personalization and interactive live viewing experiences at scale.
  • Availability: Currently in limited availability with key customers, with general launch scheduled for early next year.

Security, Authentication & AI Security Mesh

  • Evolution of Security Risks:
      • Initial LLM deployments faced prompt injection and data disclosure risks.
      • AI-written code introduced supply chain risks, including malicious packages targeting build systems.
      • Strict sandboxing models introduced too much developer friction until vendor-backed enterprise controls were adopted.
  • Biometric Authentication: Added facial, voice, and fingerprint recognition to Oracle Identity and Access Management to verify human-in-the-loop approvals.
  • Agentic Identity & Access Control: Introduced agent identity management supporting rapid creation/deletion, minimal just-in-time access, and specific approval workflows.
  • Oracle AI Security Mesh: A new product enabling companies to use advanced cyber models to continuously discover system vulnerabilities, test attack paths, and deploy automated fixes.
  • Rob Clark's Perspective: Noted that AI is not creating bugs but rather helping uncover decades-old security issues, predicting fewer patches and more secure software over the long term.

Infrastructure, Accelerators & Power Management

  • AI Accelerators & Hardware Integration:
      • Nvidia Systems: Spans from P100 (2017) and H100 (2023) to Grace Blackwell rack-scale compute with NVLink, alongside delivery of the first Vera Rubin systems to customers.
      • Alternative Processors: Bringing Google TPUs (starting with TPU V8) to OCI, AMD MI300 and 455 rack-scale offerings, Positron Atlas (power-efficient inferencing), Broadcom-OpenAI co-designed Jalapeno chips, and Cerebus/Samonova low-latency inferencing.
  • Acceleron Networking: Features multi-planar front-end and back-end networks that provide full redundancy, higher resiliency, and RDMA over front-end networks.
  • Storage Partnerships: First-party NetApp integration in OCI alongside a partner program for AI-accelerated storage (Weta, Vast, DDN) connected via Acceleron and RDMA.
  • Power Infrastructure & Grid Stability:
      • Praep Vincent highlighted that AI clusters act as massive, highly coordinated electrical loads.
      • Utilizes large energy stores (batteries and supercapacitors) combined with sophisticated power electronics as a "giant shock absorber" to smooth out power ramps and prevent grid destabilization.

Data Ecosystem, Apex & Enterprise Workflows

  • Deep Trust AI Architecture: Pushes data controls down into the database to individual tables, rows, and columns for automated policy enforcement regardless of user or agent.
  • Oracle Apex & Apex Lang: Distills enterprise applications into a high-level language ("Apex Lang") that allows AI coding agents to build applications within a safe, governed, Oracle-maintained runtime.
  • Oracle App Composer: Combines Codex code generation with IT governance, profiles, and deployment controls to turn business ideas into enterprise applications.
  • Oracle AI Workspace: Developed in partnership with OpenAI, it unifies Oracle and third-party data (Slack, Office 365, wikis) to answer questions and drive complex workflows across the enterprise.
  • Startup Bundle: Combines NetSuite QuickStart financial software, OCI credits, and OpenAI credits for new businesses.

Partnership Insights: Clay Magourk & Sam Altman

  • Model Adoption & Productivity:
      • Sam Altman noted that GPT-6 adoption has been OpenAI's strongest ever, advancing rapidly from grade school math to hard unproven math problems and enterprise software generation.
      • Clay Magourk shared that more than 70% of Oracle's software is now written by AI via Codex.
      • Altman described the modern developer experience as managing a factory inside a computer, unlocking software creation for non-technical workers.
  • AI-Driven Innovation: Altman highlighted that AI is accelerating scientific discovery and hardware design, noting that OpenAI used AI to design the Jalapeno chip faster than previously imaginable.

Notable Quotes

*"The last six months have felt like a very accelerated version... GPT-6 was one of those moments where I think the models took a step forward all at once both in terms of their ability to discover new knowledge to do very hard things but also for people's ability to work with the models."* — Sam Altman
*"My experience now sitting in front of a computer using the latest tools is... like I am managing a factory inside of my computer and I can get things that I don't enjoy doing to be automated."* — Sam Altman
*"AI is not creating the problem. AI is not creating these bugs. AI is helping us discover all of the security issues that we've have developed over the past 50 years."* — Clay Magourk

Now, the first thing you got to do is adjust your pillows. if you don't adjust your pillows properly, this whole thing is going to fall apart. okay. So, security, it's evolving rapidly. I feel like AI has been changing a lot and security has had to adapt right along with it. We've gone through multiple transitions. Why don't we start kind of back at the beginning when really we were just using AI to answer questions for us. How did that have an impact on security? I think one of the excuse me, I think one of the most interesting things on that journey was learning that new technologies can bring back old problems. initially LLMs were put in support type roles where they were given access to internal documents and training, the same sorts of things that your support staff would have access to. And pretty quickly, bad actors realized they could convince these models to disclose secrets and discount codes, say things that you as a a company would never say to your customers. And this was really an old security lesson which was don't trust user input and don't give frontline systems access to sensitive information. That makes sense. And then models continue to evolve and suddenly we're using these new AI, you know, amazing AI models to write software that just fundamentally changes the risk profile. So what do we do in that scenario? Well, again, it sort of came down to trust. But now we realize that we can't trust code even from developers that we know and trust that have been through our training. And we actually learned this with an early wakeup call where some AI included a malicious package and something that was destined for production. This actually targeted our build systems seeking to steal keys and secrets and that sort of material. we caught it with our supply chain security systems, but it was a a really stark reminder that even your build and test systems now can't trust the code that's coming in even if it's from a developer that you trust. makes complete sense and then we kind of move forward to the next step where reasoning models were suddenly much more capable and we thought I know what we should do. We should give those same models access to all the tools. left unchecked seems like it's going to create a lot of problems. What do we do about that? Well, we knew by now that trust was key, right? So, we wanted to make sure we didn't have to trust these things at all. We built a strong sandbox environment with virtualization, instrumentation, really detailed logging. It was really secure. That sounds amazing. How how did that work out? Not very well at all. our developers really didn't like using it. It introduced way too much friction and took away almost all of the benefits. Okay. The AI Boom Will Pull Back, Warns Former SEC Chair Gary Gensler

Bloomberg Podcasts(602.0K)·13:19·14.8K views·2026-10-27
Gary GenslerFormer SEC Chair Tom KeeneHost, Bloomberg Surveillance Paul SweeneyHost, Bloomberg Surveillance
TL;DRFormer SEC Chair Gary Gensler warns that the AI investment boom is a late-stage capital expenditure cycle heading for a deceleration and reversal within 18 to 30 months, with potential financial stability risks tied to over $600 billion in new debt, off-balance-sheet guarantees, and rapid chip obsolescence.
Historical Parallels: AI investment mirrors past overfunded capital cycles like railroads, canals, and the internet. Capital Scale: Companies are raising massive sums, such as Meta's $90 billion equity offering and Broadcom's recent $63 billion package. Off-Balance-Sheet...

TL;DR: Former SEC Chair Gary Gensler warns that the AI investment boom is a late-stage capital expenditure cycle heading for a deceleration and reversal within 18 to 30 months, with potential financial stability risks tied to over $600 billion in new debt, off-balance-sheet guarantees, and rapid chip obsolescence.

AI Boom, Capital Exuberance, and Overbuilding

  • Historical Parallels: AI investment mirrors past overfunded capital cycles like railroads, canals, and the internet.
    • Capital Scale: Companies are raising massive sums, such as Meta's $90 billion equity offering and Broadcom's recent $63 billion package.
    • Off-Balance-Sheet Risk: Broadcom, Nvidia, and AMD are utilizing complex guarantees and setting up special purpose vehicles reminiscent of pre-2008 financial structures, sparking concerns similar to the "08 off-balance-sheet craze."
  • Hardware Depreciation: The heavy funding is concentrated in memory chips and hardware with useful lives of 4 to 7 years.
    • Due to rapid scaling and technological advances, chips built in 2026 risk losing substantial value by 2032.
  • Productivity vs. Labor Disruptions: MIT faculty hold diverse views on AI, ranging from expectations of modest productivity gains alongside labor disruptions to optimism about broader productivity gains over the next 5 to 10 years, even amid data center overbuilding.

Financial Stability and Shadow Debt Risks

  • Insurance Sector Exposure: Over $500 billion in new debt has been raised this year for AI, pushing near $600 billion when factoring in Broadcom's $63 billion package.
    • Holding Risk: The insurance sector has heavily picked up this debt either directly or through private credit.
  • Regulatory Approach: The U.S. capital markets successfully maintain a merit-neutral stance, allowing public investment in companies like Anthropic provided there are material disclosures of all risks.
  • Central Bank Implications: The Federal Reserve must factor a potential AI deceleration into future inflation and growth projections for 2027 and 2028.

Monetary Policy and Macroeconomic Outlook

  • Federal Reserve Path: Gary Gensler anticipates a rate increase in December, but questions how the Fed will adjust monetary policy afterward if a capex boom deceleration materializes, potentially leading to fewer rate hikes in 2027.
  • Nominal GDP and Fiscal Pressures: Continuous fiscal stimuli (including the Biden stimulus) risk creating pain and angst as nominal GDP growth is reined in.
  • U.S. vs. France Fiscal Room: While the U.S. possesses limited fiscal room, it is a larger and more significant economy compared to France, which faces even higher debt-to-economy ratios without the same fiscal room to intervene.

Politics, Elections, and the Next Two Years

  • Midterms and Presidential Race: The upcoming midterm elections and early 2028 presidential campaign announcements (potentially starting January or February) will shift political dynamics.
  • Lame-Duck Dynamics: History indicates presidents in their final two years turn more toward foreign affairs and hold less leverage with Congress.
    • Bipartisan Priorities: Democrats gaining leverage in the House or Senate could still collaborate with the executive branch on voter-focused issues, including affordability, housing, AI safety, and youth tech addiction.

AI and Human Cognition at MIT

  • Student Adoption: 98% of MIT graduate and undergraduate students use AI regularly, alongside faculty usage.
  • Cognitive Risks: Humans are biologically wired to conserve energy and avoid exerting their brains, creating a risk of cognitive decline if people offload critical thinking entirely to AI.
  • Critical Thinking: Success requires treating AI as a constructive bolt-on to critical thinking skills rather than a cognitive crutch.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • AI Deceleration Timeline: Gary Gensler predicts a deceleration of the AI boom followed by some form of reversal within 18 to 30 months.
  • Presidential Announcements: Gary Gensler predicts that 15 to 20 candidates for the 2028 presidential election will announce their runs by January or February of next year (or by the Wednesday after the upcoming election).
  • Federal Reserve Rate Actions: Gary Gensler predicts the Federal Reserve will implement a rate increase in December, but may slow or pause rate hikes thereafter as economic deceleration takes hold.

The AI School Kids Love More Than Vacation | Alpha School's Joe Liemandt

a16z(305.0K)·56:01·3.5K views·2026-10-27
Joe LiemandtPrincipal, Alpha School Erik TorenbergHost, a16z Gagan BiyaniCo-founder, Horowitz Andreessen Academy
TL;DRDriven by cognitive science and large language models (LLMs), Alpha School delivers core academics in just two hours a day—allowing students to learn 10 times faster while spending the rest of their day mastering real-world life skills like teamwork and entrepreneurship.
In this discussion, Alpha School Principal Joe Liemandt, a16z podcast host Erik Torenberg, and Horowitz Andreessen Academy (HAA) Co-founder Gagan Biyani explore how this model is being adapted for college-age students to prioritize agency, high-ambition mentorship, and building...

TL;DR

Driven by cognitive science and large language models (LLMs), Alpha School delivers core academics in just two hours a day—allowing students to learn 10 times faster while spending the rest of their day mastering real-world life skills like teamwork and entrepreneurship. In this discussion, Alpha School Principal Joe Liemandt, a16z podcast host Erik Torenberg, and Horowitz Andreessen Academy (HAA) Co-founder Gagan Biyani explore how this model is being adapted for college-age students to prioritize agency, high-ambition mentorship, and building real-world products over traditional lecture halls.

Reimagining K-12: The Alpha School Model

  • The 2-Hour Academic Engine: Traditional schooling methods have stagnated for 100 years. By leveraging modern learning science and LLMs, Alpha has engineered a system where students complete all core academics in 2 hours a day.
    • Academic Performance: Students learn twice as much as they would in a standard 6-to-7-hour school day plus homework, making them top performers on standardized tests.
    • Precision Engagement: The learning engine keeps students in an 80 to 85% correct zone, providing an unending stream of personalized lessons and scaffolding that prevents boredom or burnout.
  • Radical Student Happiness: By focusing on high standards and challenging group work, 90% of Alpha students report loving school, with 50% overall (and 65% at one specific campus) stating they would rather attend school than go on vacation.
  • Rigorous Life Skills Curriculum: The remainder of the school day is dedicated to five critical life categories: leadership/teamwork, entrepreneurship/financial literacy, socialization/relationship building, public speaking, and grit.
    • Escape Room Benchmarking: Third graders are miked up in escape rooms to measure teamwork via AI transcript analysis; students must hit 85% team-oriented language to graduate the workshop.
    • Wharton MBA Challenge: Fourth and fifth graders are required to pass the Wharton MBA simulation challenge (which 50% of adult MBAs fail) as part of their core requirements.
  • Scaling with Quality: Since taking over as principal 4 years ago, Liemandt has productized the school model, scaling from Austin to 50 campuses while test scores, student happiness, and workshop quality improve annually.

Reinventing Higher Education & the HAA Model

  • The Broken University Format: Speaking to visiting interns from MIT, Stanford, and UT, Liemandt found that only 1 student out of more than 70 felt their university was properly preparing them for post-graduation life in the age of AI. Large lecture classes have attendance dropping below 10%, with students viewing them as a charade.
  • Horowitz Andreessen Academy (HAA) Design Principles: Gagan Biyani explains that HAA applies similar modern principles to college-age students by focusing heavily on student success rather than adult politics.
    • The 80-Hour Internship Test: Summer interns at Alpha worked 80 hours a week researching learning science and building educational small language models (SLMs) to make them free for global sovereigns, proving that motivated students thrive when building real products.
    • High-Ambition Travel and Autonomy: HAA allocates $5,000 per student for travel budgets, encouraging solo travel to countries with less than $20,000 GDP per capita to build global perspective and resilience.
  • The Value of Network and Proximity: Drawing inspiration from early institutions like the Teal Fellowship, HAA curates its applicant pool tightly, exposing students to legendary builders (such as Jensen Huang, Sam Altman, and Satya Nadella) to raise their personal ambitions.

Entrepreneurship and the Future of Work

  • Founder School: Alpha launched a specialized high school focused entirely on entrepreneurship, recognizing that AI will drive a massive explosion in independent business creation.
  • The Million-Dollar Guarantee: Founder School guarantees that students will start a business earning $1 million in profit while in high school, or tuition is refunded. Students are currently speed-running the curriculum as freshmen.
  • The Power of Cold Outreach: Highlighting student agency, Liemandt shared the story of a 14-year-old student who cold-called Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. While Huang critiqued the student's call preparation (giving him an "F" on research), he took the call and gave him mentorship—turning it into a transformative life lesson.

Overcoming Parental Resistance and Cultural Inertia

  • The 1910 Farm Tractor Analogy: Liemandt compares the current educational inflection point to 1910, when the introduction of the farm tractor made 30% of agricultural jobs obsolete, prompting 130,000 American towns to vote to raise taxes to fund a brand-new high school system.
  • Adoption Curves: Opening new campuses often starts with small cohorts (e.g., opening New York City with 10 to 12 students), but demand skyrockets once local families witness the actual performance and happiness of the first cohorts.
  • Product Definition: Liemandt stresses that schools must clearly define their product—attracting families who embrace modern tools and repelling laggards who reject screens or AI.

The airline CEO putting Starlink on every plane

Cheeky Pint(21.5K)·79:48·9.8K views·2026-10-27
Scott KirbyCEO, United Airlines John ThompsonHost, Cheeky Pint Ben ThompsonHost, Cheeky Pint
TL;DRScott Kirby, CEO of United Airlines, details the airline’s contrarian pandemic strategy of massive fleet and technology expansion, the rollout of SpaceX Starlink across every plane, and why customer surveys and traditional metrics fail in the airline business.
Pandemic Wide-Body Order: During COVID when travel experts predicted demand would drop 50% forever and global competitors permanently retired wide-body fleets, Scott Kirby and United did the opposite, executing the largest aircraft order in history: 200 wide-bodies and several...

TL;DR

Scott Kirby, CEO of United Airlines, details the airline’s contrarian pandemic strategy of massive fleet and technology expansion, the rollout of SpaceX Starlink across every plane, and why customer surveys and traditional metrics fail in the airline business.

Fleet Expansion & Contrarian Bets

  • Pandemic Wide-Body Order: During COVID when travel experts predicted demand would drop 50% forever and global competitors permanently retired wide-body fleets, Scott Kirby and United did the opposite, executing the largest aircraft order in history: 200 wide-bodies and several hundred narrow-bodies.
  • Capital Discipline vs. Short-Termism: United invested heavily during the downturn to secure 30-year assets at advantageous pricing, mirroring cyclical manufacturing strategies like Samsung's memory investments.
  • Market Dominance in Hubs: Scott Kirby highlights that single-hub operations (like Atlanta and Dallas) drive more international service and economic activity than fragmented hubs like Chicago, where United and American Airlines duplicate 16 daily flights to Milwaukee.

Technology Stack & In-House Software

  • Core Tech Modernization: In 2020, United invested hundreds of millions to move off mainframes onto the cloud and build modern databases, enabling fast execution that competitors cannot replicate.
  • In-House Reservation Legacy: United is one of only two airlines globally (alongside Copa Airlines) that owns its own reservation system, refusing to outsource to Sabre or Apollo.
  • Seat-Back Map & Entertainment Redesign: United is taking over map software from Panasonic to eliminate annoying globe views and build personalized preferences and proactive milestone memories (such as a live countdown to a passenger's 2 millionth mile).
  • AI Vibe Coding: After taking three 1-hour tutoring sessions, Scott Kirby independently wrote a sports betting aggregation web app in 2.5 hours at his home gym, sparking an internal AI push.

Starlink Rollout & Competitive Differentiation

  • Speed Over "Free": United chose SpaceX's Starlink not because it is free, but because fast bandwidth is the true value proposition; legacy free Wi-Fi is compared to slow dial-up internet.
  • Fleet Rollout Pace: United is currently at 48% of flights equipped with Starlink, upgrading about three planes per day and planning to reach 50% within a week and 100% in hubs like Los Angeles in the first half of next year.
  • Targeting Competitors: Scott Kirby views Starlink as a temporary multi-year competitive advantage that will drive passengers from competing airlines (including Delta and joint-venture partner ANA) to try United and stay for its superior overall technology.

Operational Hurdles & Air Traffic Control

  • ATC as the Primary Bottleneck: Air traffic control accounts for 90% of airline operational issues, constrained by inefficient annual appropriations processes and political distribution of 450 regional facilities.
  • Weather Simulation: United is using AI simulations for Denver summer pop-up thunderstorms to potentially reduce weather-impacted delays by 50%–75%.
  • Low-Cost Carrier Failures: Low-cost carriers like Spirit Airlines failed because cost inflation and exorbitant airport authority fees (such as $52 per passenger in New York airports) made their business model mathematically unsustainable.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Hub-Specific Starlink Target: Scott Kirby stated that Los Angeles will be United's first 100% Starlink-equipped hub in the first half of next year.
  • Weather Delay Reductions: Scott Kirby claimed that AI simulations can reduce weather-impacted delays in Denver by 50% to 75% in the summer.
  • Industry Financial Outlook: Scott Kirby predicted that 6 out of 10 US airlines will lose money this year.

Notable Quotes

"The experts live in the world of two standard deviations. They force-fit everything into the things that they've already seen. When things happen outside two standard deviations, when the tails happen, which is where you create all the value… That's where I first started." — Scott Kirby
"Anyone that says the word weather in my presence is going to get fired, because I do not want to use weather as an excuse. I know weather happens... but our job is to figure out how to minimize the impact of weather." — Scott Kirby
"Our constraint on investing in technology is how much can we manage? It is not money." — Scott Kirby
I'm talking to people like, "I'm switching to flying a regional flight because it's got Starlink." I was like, "Holy." Free was never the value proposition. Fast is the value proposition, and there's only one option for fast. It's Starlink. That is the only option. Scott Kirby took over United Airlines in May 2020 at the height of the pandemic. Refusing to play defense, he's made massive bets on fleet expansion, premium seats, and Starlink Wi-Fi, creating differentiation in the hugely competitive airline market. You did a big fleet order during COVID that looked risky. Was it risky? I didn't think it was. Back during COVID, every single expert in the world said travel demand is going to be down by 50% forever. That's what everyone said. One other point, you should ignore the experts outside of two sigma. In the tails, the experts are terrible. I just thought they were all wrong. I thought COVID's going to be deep and last a long time, and then travel demand will recover. Everyone else around the globe was shrinking, so we did the exact opposite. Literally, every global long-haul airline in the world, except for United, permanently retired part of their wide-body fleets. We looked at that and said, "We think the premise is wrong, and it's just a huge opportunity to do the opposite," do the biggest aircraft order in history, which we did. I remember telling our board when they asked, they said the same thing: "This seems like a really big risk. Those are airplanes that list for… We get a big discount, but they list for $400 million. Sure. I told our board, "This is one of those cases because everyone else is shrinking, we don't have to be right. We just have to be not totally wrong." It's just one of those situations where the odds are so stacked in your favor. People out here in Silicon Valley do this stuff all the time where... Figure out something that you have a different view than everyone else and go big on it. If it works, the returns are huge, and the downsides are smaller. There's been this cycle with large capital purchases. It's actually also a tech story that's relevant with memory. Samsung came to dominate memory by systematically investing into every downturn because it's very cyclical. Obviously, it's hugely beneficial today. The flip side of that is everyone wants to invest when it's at its peak. Have we gone full circle? You want to invest when everyone else... When things are down, and not invest when things are up. Because we were the only one in the world. We re-launched the market. For Boeing, they could go to other customers and say, "Look, United believes. You should, too." I remember... It's very striking. I remember that period of people over-extrapolating COVID. The tech version of that was companies deciding that they're going to be fully remote forever, and a lot of companies decided that, and then were like, "Wait a second. No, that's not right." Why were people so wrong on the travel side of things? There are two things that are human nature that are mistakes. One is to extrapolate the current into the future. Assume that everything that's happening today is going to continue into the future. Two, trusting people on surveys. Surveys are the absolute worst way to figure out what's going to happen in the future. Because the reason travel experts thought travel… They did surveys, and people were terrified. Everyone's dying. You ask them, "Are you going to travel internationally again?" "No!" That was true what they thought at the time. But once it was over, of course, they were going to go back to traveling again. By the way, I said this earlier. Experts live in the world of two standard deviations. They force-fit everything into the things that they've already seen. When things happen outside two standard deviations, when the tails happen, which is where you create all the value… It's famously in finance, the frequency of six-sigma events is… That's where I first started. VaR, Value at Risk, is the dumbest model in history. It's stupid. Things that shouldn't happen in the history of the universe happened once every five years. Because it's a dumb, dumb, fundamentally flawed mathematical model that assumes everything is normally distributed. It's not. It's just the perfect... I had a debate when I was getting my master's degree back in the '90s. This was about Black-Scholes. They're such a dumb model for valuing options because it assumes a normal distribution. The world is not normally distributed. The tails are much fatter in everything that matters. Anyway, that applies to everything but especially in a crisis. Black-Scholes has a lot of 'assume a spherical cow' assumptions baked into it. Okay, I love hearing you talk about the over-fixation on surveys. The travel industry, the airline industry, loves surveys. United probably surveys people up the wazoo. We're trying to encourage people to… Yes. Your opinions should survive contact with reality, but the metrics are not going to tell you what to do. We use survey… I like surveys for how did people feel about stuff we've already done. I ignore surveys for what are we going to do in the future. Apple famously does not do a lot of focus group testing on their products. They just build the products they want to build. That's the way... Stuff that we're doing right now. Today, we rolled out a Starlink tracker. I didn't ask anyone about that. I sit on an airplane and talk to customers, and I know they're going to think that's cool. And they do think it's cool. When you're getting to the second thing, which is people try to over-measure tiny steps, and it's just like, "Do the Starlink tracker. If it's that useful, whatever, makes this better." Especially in a world of AI, you can do so much so fast. It's really powerful. What's Still Valuable When Intelligence Is Free

Invest Like The Best(113.0K)·71:28·19.8K views·2026-10-27
Cliff SosinInvestor Patrick O'ShaughnessyHost, Invest Like The Best
TL;DRCliff Sosin and Patrick discuss why the infinite scaling of AI intelligence faces fundamental mathematical and physical limits, arguing that when intelligence becomes free and ubiquitous, the true winners will be businesses owning scarce complements like data, attention, and physical scale.
The "Roundness of a Ball" Metaphor: Cliff Sosin explains that intelligence is not an unbounded scalar (like tree height) but a ratio—a gadget used to interpolate between facts. Mathematics vs. Reality: While math can be solved perfectly, most interesting real-world domains...

TL;DR

Cliff Sosin and Patrick discuss why the infinite scaling of AI intelligence faces fundamental mathematical and physical limits, arguing that when intelligence becomes free and ubiquitous, the true winners will be businesses owning scarce complements like data, attention, and physical scale.

The Limits of Intelligence: Complex Dynamic Systems vs. Raw IQ

  • The "Roundness of a Ball" Metaphor: Cliff Sosin explains that intelligence is not an unbounded scalar (like tree height) but a ratio—a gadget used to interpolate between facts.
      • *Mathematics vs. Reality:* While math can be solved perfectly, most interesting real-world domains are complex, non-linear dynamic systems (like weather or human interaction).
      • *The Butterfly Effect:* Small errors in initial conditions compound exponentially over time. Even super-intelligent models cannot perfectly predict complex dynamic systems without touching reality via physical experiments.
  • Speed Limits of Physical Experimentation: While automated biology labs and robotics closing the feedback loop are revolutionary, they remain bound by physical reality (e.g., mice taking a year to grow up and die).

Investing Beyond Cheap Intelligence: Complements to Intelligence

  • The "What's Left?" Framework: When assuming everyone has access to 200-IQ intelligence in their pocket, investors must ask what cannot be commoditized.
      • Scarcce Complements: The winners are owners of data, attention, emotional attachment/love, physical proximity, scale, and regulatory capture.
  • Meta Case Study: Meta spends its massive $250 billion revenue/costs largely on intelligence (data centers, inference, engineers). As intelligence costs collapse by a factor of 100, Meta's costs drop while creators and advertisers use cheap intelligence to make vastly superior content and targeted funnels—all anchored by Meta's un-destroyable assets: massive proprietary data and user attention.
  • Small Business Expansion: Companies previously constrained by team size (e.g., Grindr with attention and data, but limited engineering headcount) could theoretically leverage massive external intelligence to build out features previously impossible for their scale.

Commoditization of Frontier Labs and the Jevons Paradox

  • Diamond-Shaped Demand Frontier: As models improve, chatbot and customer service use cases rapidly commoditize. Elite intelligence (e.g., 300-IQ tasks) has a surprisingly narrow addressable market compared to "good enough" intelligence.
  • The Fiber Optic Analogy: Cliff Sosin compares intelligence to communication bandwidth: after a massive adoption boom driven by Jevons paradox, the cost of moving information kept falling while the overall industry market size shrank dramatically (e.g., Cogent Communications).
  • Frontier Labs Outlook: Even if labs produce the cheapest frontier tokens, they face rampant deflation and shrinking high-margin use cases if "good enough" intelligence becomes dirt cheap.

The Infrastructure Build-Out and Capital Cycles

  • The Great Household Analogy: Cliff Sosin likens current American society to a household saving for a house—feeling pinched because vast resources (electricians, plumbers, capital) are diverted to build AI infrastructure.
  • Causality of the Boom: The massive infrastructure build-out is only happening because there is a strong price signal and top talent chasing it; investors trying to forecast inference supply and demand face extreme uncertainty across multiple orders of magnitude.
  • Capital Markets Agility: Cliff Sosin praises U.S. capital markets for remarkably redeploying ~3% of GDP into speculative AI infrastructure on a turn of a dime—a feat unmatched globally, rooted in a cultural and institutional tolerance for risk-taking.

AI Agents, Market Pricing, and Portfolio Management

  • Dynamic Pricing and Elimination of Friction: AI agents will constantly optimize everyday choices (e.g., automatically switching garbage collection providers or bank deposit rates based on marginal cost savings). This will eliminate price stickiness and cause local prices to fluctuate rapidly.
  • Transformation of Enterprise Systems: Transforming legacy companies like Carvana requires starting with foundational steps: "vibe coding" hackathons for all employees to understand what is possible, demanding read-only API access to every internal system for AI agents, and progressively unlocking write and system-change permissions.
  • Portfolio Concentration: Defending his own highly concentrated portfolio (heavy in Carvana), Cliff Sosin argues that standard academic literature favoring extreme diversification is designed for average results, whereas historical outliers capturing generational business advantages (like Walmart or Home Depot early on) relied on high concentration.

Education and the Future of Human Skill

  • Bimodal Education: Cliff Sosin argues that personal intelligence remains vital because you need a baseline of crystallized knowledge (e.g., understanding stochastic processes or matrix multiplication) to effectively direct and prompt AI models.
  • Ambitious Prompting: Rather than banning AI on Shakespeare essays, educators should assign tasks 100 times harder and more ambitious, forcing students to deeply understand, own, and justify the AI-generated work product.

Notable Quotes

  • > *"If you think of intelligence as like an unbounded scalar like the height of a tree isn't correct. What you should really think about intelligence as is more like a ratio like the roundness of a ball."* — Cliff Sosin
  • > *"If intelligence becomes cheap and ubiquitous, what you want to own are the complements to intelligence. And so the complements to intelligence are data, attention, like love, affection... physical proximity, scale, and regulatory capture."* — Cliff Sosin
  • > *"You can't actually... for mixing cream into your coffee or predicting the weather or thinking about how groups of people... are going to interact with each other, intelligence just isn't that powerful."* — Cliff Sosin
