Yann LeCun, AMI Labs | Evident AI Symposium 2026

Evident Insights(252)·19:26·2026-10-27
Yann LeCunExecutive Chairman, AMI Labs Alexandra MousavizadehCo-Founder and Co-CEO, Evident
TL;DRYann LeCun explains that while Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at symbolic tasks, they fundamentally lack the ability to reason, plan, and operate in the physical world; he argues that joint embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) and world models represent the necessary next paradigm for achieving true physical AI and robotics.
Corporate Setup: Yann LeCun explains that AMI Labs (pronounced Anidance, meaning "friend" in French) is headquartered in Paris with major offices in New York and smaller offices in Montreal and Singapore. - Funding & Scale: The company started operations in March, raised...

TL;DR

Yann LeCun explains that while Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at symbolic tasks, they fundamentally lack the ability to reason, plan, and operate in the physical world; he argues that joint embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) and world models represent the necessary next paradigm for achieving true physical AI and robotics.

AMI Labs & Company Overview

  • Corporate Setup: Yann LeCun explains that AMI Labs (pronounced *Anidance*, meaning "friend" in French) is headquartered in Paris with major offices in New York and smaller offices in Montreal and Singapore.
  • Funding & Scale: The company started operations in March, raised approximately $1 billion in a seed round (believed to be the largest seed round in history outside the US), and currently employs around 65 people.
  • Core Mission: Focused entirely on building AI systems for the real physical world ("physical AI") through the creation of world models.

Limitations of LLMs & The Moravec Paradox

  • The Core Fallacy: LeCun notes a 70-year-old recurring mistake in AI where researchers assume that if a machine performs a complex human intellectual task (e.g., playing chess, proving theorems, or passing the bar exam), it possesses general or superintelligence.
  • The Moravec Paradox: Machines easily manipulate discrete symbols, code, and mathematics (via LLMs), but struggle immensely with the real world, which is messy, continuous, high-dimensional, noisy, and unpredictable.
  • Real-World Failures: Despite LLM advancements, the industry still lacks domestic robots and Level 5 self-driving cars that can learn to drive in a few hours like a teenager.

World Models & JEPA Architecture

  • Definition of a World Model: A system that starts with an idea of the state of the world at time $t$, predicts the resulting state at time $t+1$ after taking a specific action, and uses optimization against a cost function to plan a sequence of actions.
  • Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA): Unlike generative models that try to predict every pixel or token (which fails in high-noise environments like video or financial time series), JEPA learns an abstract representation of the input and predicts only what is predictable while ignoring unpredictable noise.
  • Analogy to Finance and Physics: Likened to financial data (mostly noise with a little information) and scientific modeling (such as using the ideal gas law or Navier-Stokes equations without needing to simulate every individual air molecule).
  • Hierarchical World Models: Published a paper last week showing how high-level goals (e.g., traveling from New York to Paris) break down into hierarchical sub-goals (hailing a taxi, walking out a door) because short-term predictions are accurate while long-term physical details are uncertain.

Global Landscape & Competitiveness

  • Asian Leadership in Physical AI: Countries like China, Japan, Korea, and Taiwan are heavily pushing robotics and physical AI due to their strong manufacturing foundations.
  • Shift from Silicon Valley: Because LLMs are inadequate for physical robotics, many researchers in Asia (particularly China) are rapidly publishing and working on world models and joint embedding approaches, outpacing Silicon Valley, which LeCun describes as "hypnotized by LLMs."

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Yann LeCun predicts that AMI Labs will achieve success in various industries over the next year.

Notable Quotes

"There is a mistake that people in AI and at the periphery of AI have kept making for the last 70 years. And it's the mistake that if a machine can do a complex task or a task identified by humans as intellectually complicated, then automatically this machine is smarter than humans..." — Yann LeCun
"Much financial data is mostly noise with a little bit of information in it. If you train the system to do sub time series prediction of financial data, it has to be able to predict its own uncertainty. You cannot train it to predict a particular outcome because it's mostly noise." — Yann LeCun
"...Silicon Valley, which seems completely hypnotized by LLMs and kind of too entrenched in LLMs to kind of look around." — Yann LeCun

So, what is a world model? In in our opinion, a lot of people have different meanings for that, but what we mean by world model is that if you have an idea of the state of the world at time t and you imagine taking an action, your world model will predict the resulting state of the world at time t plus one after you have taken this action. And if you have such a world model, you can use it to plan a sequence of actions to accomplish a task. Okay, so a task would be some sort of cost function that indicates whether the task has been accomplished or not. If it's accomplished, the cost is zero. If it's not accomplished, it's a a higher cost. And by optimization, you can figure out a sequence of actions that will accomplish the task according to the predictions of your world model. Okay, this is very classical in the optimal control and motion planning and things like that. except that what we the the type of world model we would use, our world models are not trained from from data as opposed to being a virtual equation that we turn by scientists or engineers. so that that's the the the whole idea. The characteristic of this approach is that it's not a generative model. So, you know how when you train an NLM or another generative model like a video generation system, you you show it, let's say a piece of text if it's an NLM, and you train the system to predict the next word in the text. And of course, you can never exactly predict which word follows a particular text, but what you can do is predict a kind of probability distribution over all possible words in your dictionary. Okay, so in a discrete world, handling the uncertainty in the prediction is relatively easy. It's just, you know, giving a a score to every potential outcome. Now, think about if you want to transpose that kind of method of kind of learning about a particular input modality by by training it to predict, generative prediction doesn't work. It doesn't work for video. If you If you would train a system to predict what's going to happen in the video, there's no way it can predict every detail in that video. Because most of the details in the world are completely unpredictable. You're all sitting in the room right now. If I point the camera to the left side of the room, and I slowly rotate the camera to look at the right side, I stop the camera there, and I ask the system predict the rest of the video, system will predict probably that the camera is going to keep panning. There is absolutely no way it can predict what every person on this on the right side of the room looks like. Because the information is just not there. So, when you train a generative system, which is trying to predict every detail in the input at the pixel level in the case of video, you basically kill it. and so, you have to invent a a new set of technique for this. And by the way, this applies to financial data, right? If you much financial data is mostly noise with a little bit of information in it. If you do if you train the system to do sub time series prediction of financial data, it has to be able to predict its own uncertainty. You cannot train it to predict a particular outcome because because it's mostly noise. so, so that this the same the same part and so, what what can you do? The the the technology that we've developed and we continue developing at NeurIPS is one in which the system learns an abstract representation of the input and it makes prediction in that abstract representation space. So, it does not have time to even all the details of the observation. It tries to predict whatever is predictable. But ignore all the details that are simply not not predictable. And I would think this is absolutely fundamental. And and we do this all the time in in in modeling. For example, let me take a simple example. if I want to let's say I'm a aircraft design engineer and I want to design an airfoil for an airplane. I can I can simulate the the the flow of the air around the the airplane using Navier-Stokes equations. But that's actually an approximation. It's a it's it's a an abstract representation of the underlying phenomenon, which is molecules of air bumping into each other and onto the airplane. But we never simulate this at that level because it's just too many details that we could not actually simulate accurately anyway. So, we invent an abstract representation that allows us to make predictions. Similarly, if I give you a box a closed box with air in it and I tell you I'm going to heat up this box by 10 degrees. You can predict exactly by how much the pressure will increase because you have the ideal gas law PV=nRT. But there's no way you can predict the trajectory of every molecule of air in that box. So so this idea that, you know, we need to invent abstract representation of reality to allow us to make prediction is really fundamental. Which is why you cannot reduce chemistry to physics or or you know atomic physics to particle physics or or or biochemistry to chemistry or biology to biochemistry, right?
